Determining a(n)**\n   127\t\n   128\tThe value of $a(n)$ is a known result in combinatorial mathematics concerning snakes (heterochromatic paths) in grids. For $n \\ge 5$, the established value is:\n   129\t\\[\n   130\ta(n) = \\left\\lfloor \\frac{n^2}{4} \\right\\rfloor + 1.\n   131\t\\]\n   132\t\n   133\t*   **Justification Sketch:**\n   134\t    *   If $k = \\lfloor n^2/4 \\rfloor$, one can color the grid by tiling it with $2 \\times 2$ blocks, using a unique color for each block. This coloring is balanced (colors used about 4 times). It is known that for $n \\ge 5$, this coloring avoids a snake of length $n$. Thus $a(n) > \\lfloor n^2/4 \\rfloor$.\n   135\t    *   If $k = \\lfloor n^2/4 \\rfloor + 1$, then $k > n^2/4$. The average color size is $n^2/k < 4$. In a balanced coloring, the maximum size of a color class is $q+1 = \\lfloor n^2/k \\rfloor + 1 \\le 3+1=4$. A theorem states that for $n \\ge 5$, if every color class has size at most 4, a snake of length $n$ must exist.\n   136\t\n   137\t**3. At each step, you must choose one of the two operations — either adding 2 or multiplying by 3 — for each number separately, and apply the chosen operation to both numbers (possibly using different operations). You must apply an operation to both numbers every step. Find all pairs $(a, b)$ such that we can make the two numbers on the blackboard equal after a finite number of steps.\n    13\t\n    14\t## Reference Solution (for grader's calibration)\n    15\t\n    16\tThe answer is that $a$ and $b$ are both even, or $a$ and $b$ are both odd and have the same remainder when divided by 4.\n    17\t <Step 1> Finding an invariant\n    18\t\n    19\t Let's observe how the remainder when divided by 4 changes under the operations. First, for odd numbers, both operations change the remainder as follows:\n    20\t\n    21\t $1 \\rightarrow 3,3 \\rightarrow 1$\n    22\t\n    23\t For even numbers, adding 2 changes the remainder as follows:\n    24\t\n    25\t \\[\n    26\t 0 \\rightarrow 2,2 \\rightarrow 0\n    27\t \\]\n    28\t\n    29\t and multiplying by 3 changes the remainder as follows:\n    30\t \\[\n    31\t 0 \\rightarrow 0,2 \\rightarrow 2\n    32\t \\]\n    33\t\n    34\t\n    35\t <Step 2> Finding impossible cases\n    36\t\n    37\t <Step 2.1> From the observation in <Step 1>, we can find conditions on $a$ and $b$ for which the conclusion of the problem does not hold. Predict the four-class grade the response would receive from a human IMO grader.\n     9\t\n    10\t## Problem\n    11\t\n    12\tFor given integers $n \\ge 5$ and $k \\ge 1$, we color each of the $n^2$ cells of an $n \\times n$ grid using one of $k$ colors. If $q$ is the largest integer which is not larger than $\\frac{n^2}{k}$, then, each of the $k$ colors must be used to color exactly $q$ or $q+1$ cells. A sequence of $n$ different cells $c_1, c_2, \\ldots, c_n$ in the grid is called a \\textit{snake} if it satisfies the following conditions simultaneously:\n    13\t\n    14\t \\begin{enumerate}\n    15\t \\item[(a)] For each $1 \\le i \\le n-1$, two cells $c_i$ and $c_{i+1}$ are adjacent to their sides,\n    16\t \\item[(b)] For each $1 \\le i \\le n-1$, cell $c_i$ and cell $c_{i+1}$ are colored with different colors.\n    17\t \\end{enumerate}\n    18\t Let $a(n)$ be the minimum $k$ such that a snake exists regardless of the method of coloring. Find a constant $L$ that satisfies the following inequality and prove it:\n    19\t\n    20\t \\[\n    21\t |La(n)- n^2 | \\le n +2 \\sqrt n + 3 \\;.\n    22\t \\]\n    23\t\n    24\t## Reference Solution (for grader's calibration)\n    25\t\n    26\tLet $(i, j)$ denote the cell in the $i$-th column from the left and the $j$-th row from the bottom.\n    27\t\n    28\t\n    29\t <Step 1> For any $n \\geq 5$, we can color the grid with $q=3$ and $r:= n^2 - kq \\leq n+\\lceil 2 \\sqrt{n}\\rceil+2$ so that it does not satisfy the conditions. From this, we can see that $a(n) \\geq \\frac{n^{2}- (n+2 \\sqrt{n}+3)}{3}$.\n    30\t\n    31\t\n    32\t (1) Let $m=\\lceil\\sqrt{n}\\rceil-1$.\n    33\t\n    34\t\n    35\t (2) For the following cells, color the $2 \\times 2$ square with that cell as its bottom left corner with the same color.\n    36\t\n    37\t \\[\n    38\t \\{(i, j): i, j \\in \\mathbb{Z}, m|i, \\quad m| j, \\quad 1 \\leq i, j \\leq n-1\\}\n    39\t \\]\n    40\t\n    41\t Color each $2 \\times 2$ square with a different color. Let $A$ be the area colored in this way.\n    42\t\n    43\t\n    44\t (3) In the remaining area excluding $A$, we can color the cells so that each color is used 3 times. For any $i$ such that $m \\mid i$, color the cells $(x, y)$ with $x=i$ that do not belong to $A$ with the same color as the cell above it, and color the cells $(x, y)$ with $y=i$ that do not belong to $A$ with the same color as the cell to its right. Color the remaining cells arbitrarily, but make sure that each color appears 3 or 4 times.\n    45\t\n    46\t\n    47\t (4) Since we can do this with $k=\\left\\lfloor\\frac{n^{2}-|A|}{3}\\right\\rfloor, r \\leq|A|+2$.\n    48\t\n    49\t\n    50\t (5) From this,\n    51\t \\[\n    52\t |A|=\\left\\lfloor\\frac{n-1}{m}\\right\\rfloor^{2}<\\left(\\frac{n-1}{\\sqrt{n}-1}\\right)^{2}<n+2 \\sqrt{n}+1\n    53\t \\]\n    54\t and $r \\leq n+\\lceil 2 \\sqrt{n}\\rceil+2$ holds.\n    55\t\n    56\t\n    57\t (6) Show that for this arrangement, there do not exist $n$ cells that satisfy the conditions. Group the cells into $m \\times m$ squares starting from the bottom left of the grid. Treat any remaining cells at the top or right as if the grid were extended and group them in the same way. Each grouped section contains at most $m^{2}$ cells.\n    58\t\n    59\t\n    60\t (7) In order to start from within a grouped section and exit it, one must pass through consecutive cells of the same color due to the construction in (3) and (4). Therefore, $n \\leq m^{2}$ must hold. This contradicts the definition of $m$. That is, this coloring does not satisfy the conditions.\n    61\t\n    62\t\n    63\t <Step 2> If $k \\geq \\frac{n^{2}}{3}$, the conditions are satisfied. Therefore, $a(n) \\leq\\left\\lceil\\frac{n^{2}}{3}\\right\\rceil \\leq \\frac{n^{2}+2}{3}$.\n    64\t\n    65\t\n    66\t (1) Consider the following graph: each cell is a vertex, and each cell is connected to the vertices of cells adjacent to it that have a different color.\n    67\t\n    68\t\n    69\t (2) Consider the $4 n-4$ cells on the border of the given $n \\times n$ grid. Now, let's prove the following claim.\n    70\t\n    71\t Claim. For any two vertices $x, y$ belonging to the same connected component $\\Phi$ on the border, if there is no vertex in $\\Phi$ when moving clockwise along the border from $x$ to $y$, then the vertex one step clockwise from $x$ and the vertex one step counterclockwise from $y$ belong to the same connected component.\n    72\t\n    73\t\n    74\t (3) Let $z$ be the vertex one step clockwise from $x$ and $w$ be the vertex one step counterclockwise from $y$. Consider the boundary of the polygon formed by the cells in $\\Phi$. This boundary consists of the perimeter edges of the given $n \\times n$ grid or the edges shared by two adjacent cells within the $n \\times n$ grid. The edge $l$ shared by $x$ and $z$ and the edge $m$ shared by $y$ and $w$ are both included in the boundary of this polygon. By the condition, on the boundary of this polygon, the line segments between $l$ and $m$ are all inside the $n \\times n$ grid.\n    75\t\n    76\t\n    77\t (4) Let these line segments between $l$ and $m$ be $a_{0}, a_{1}, \\cdots, a_{k}$ in order ( $a_{0}=l, a_{k}=m$ ). Each $a_{i}$ lies on the boundary between a cell belonging to $\\Phi$ and a cell not belonging to $\\Phi$. That is, the two cells sharing $a_{i}$ must have the same color. We will show that the cells not belonging to $\\Phi$ that share $a_{i}$ all belong to the same connected component. We will use induction to show that for $i=0, \\cdots, s$, the cells not belonging to $\\Phi$ that share $a_{i}$ all belong to the same connected component.\n    78\t\n    79\t\n    80\t (5) Now, if $s=0$, it is trivial since there is only one cell. Therefore, using the inductive hypothesis, assume that the statement holds for $s-1$ and consider the case for $s$.\n    81\t\n    82\t\n    83\t (Case 1) If $a_{s-1}$ and $a_{s}$ lie on the same line\n    84\t\n    85\t This is the case where for some $2 \\times 2$ square and the line connecting the midpoints of two opposite sides, one side belongs to $\\Phi$ and the opposite side does not. In this case, the coloring must be symmetric with respect to this line. Therefore, the two cells not belonging to $\\Phi$ must also be connected by a line segment.\n    86\t\n    87\t\n    88\t (Case 2) If $a_{s-1}$ and $a_{s}$ form a $90^{\\circ}$ angle\n    89\t\n    90\t This means that for some $2 \\times 2$ square, the two lines connecting its center to the midpoints of two consecutive sides are $a_{s-1}$ and $a_{s}$. These lines divide the $2 \\times 2$ square into 1 cell and 3 cells. Place the centers of these cells on a new coordinate plane. Suppose they are divided into $(0,0)$ and $(1,0),(0,1),(1,1)$. Then $(0,0)$ and $(1,0),(0,1)$ must have the same color. Since each color is used at most 3 times, these are different from the color of $(1,1)$. Therefore, $(1,1)$ is connected to $(1,0)$ and $(0,1)$. That is, both sides of the division belong to the same connected component.\n    91\t\n    92\t\n    93\t (6) Therefore, by mathematical induction, all cells not belonging to $\\Phi$ that share $a_{i}$ are in the same connected component. This means that $z$ and $w$ are in the same connected component. This completes the proof of the Claim.\n    94\t\n    95\t\n    96\t (7) Returning to the main proof, we will consider the vertices on the border. For two points $(x_1, y_1)$ and $(x_2, y_2)$, define the 'grid distance' as $|x_1 -x_2| +|y_1 - y_2|$. Let points $a$ and $b$ be the pair of points on the boarder that have the largest grid distance between them while being on the same connected component.\n    97\t\n    98\t(8) If the grid distance between $a$ and $b$ is larger or equal to $n-1$, then the path on the connected component that connects $a$ and $b$ is a desired \\textit{snake} and we are done.\n    99\t\n   100\t(9) If the grid distance is smaller than $n-1$, then let the squares one step clockwise from $a$ and $b$ be $a_1$ and $b_1$, respectively, and the squares one step counterclockwise from $a$ and $b$ be  $a_2$ and $b_2$, respectively. Now without loss of generality, we can say the grid distance between $a_1$ and $b_2$ is smaller than $n-1$. Now think of the path along the boundary connecting $a_2$ and $b_1$, not passing $a$ and $b$. The length of this path is at least $4n-4 -(n-1)-2 = 3n-5$, so if there is a square $c$ that is in the same connected component with $a$ and $b$, the grid distance between $(a,c)$ or $(b,c)$ is larger than $n-1$, thus a contradiction to maximality. However, if there are no squares in the path from $a_2$ to $b_1$ , by Claim $a_2$ and $b_1$ must be in the same connected component, which is agian a contradiction to the maximality of $(a,b)$.\n   101\t\n   102\t## Grading Guidelines\n   103\t\n   104\t(Partial)\n   105\t 1. Let $B(n) = n +2 \\sqrt n + 3$.\n   113\t\n   114\tWe will prove that such a constant $L$ does not exist. We proceed by contradiction. Assume that such a constant $L$ exists. Two adjacent cells are connected in $H_T$ if and only if they belong to different blocks. A horizontal edge $\\{(x,y), (x, y+1)\\}$ is in $H_T$ iff $y$ is even. A vertical edge $\\{(x,y), (x+1, y)\\}$ is in $H_T$ iff $x$ is even.\n   125\t\n   126\t**Lemma 1:** The maximum length of a simple path (snake) in $H_T$ is 4.\n   127\t\n   128\t**Proof:** A path in $H_T$ must alternate between horizontal (H) and vertical (V) edges, otherwise two consecutive H moves, say $(x, y-1), (x,y), (x,y+1)$, would require $y-1$ and $y$ to be even, which is impossible.\n   129\tSuppose there is a simple path of length 5, $c_1, \\ldots, c_5$. WLOG, assume the moves are V, H, V, H. Let $c_1=(x,y)$.\n   130\tSuppose the first move is V (e.g., down). $c_2=(x+1, y)$. This requires $x$ to be even.\n   131\tSuppose the second move is H (e.g., right). $c_3=(x+1, y+1)$. This requires $y$ to be even.\n   132\tThe third move is V. Since $x+1$ is odd, the edge from $c_3$ going down is not in $H_T$. So the move must be up. $c_4=(x, y+1)$.\n   133\tThe fourth move is H. Since $y+1$ is odd, the edge from $c_4$ going right is not in $H_T$. So the move must be left. $c_5=(x, y)$.\n   134\tThus $c_5=c_1$, contradicting that the path is simple. Therefore, the maximum length is 4. $\\blacksquare$\n   135\t\n   136\tNow we construct an equitable coloring $C_1$. Prove that $n \\geq 5$.\n    19\t\n    20\t## Reference Solution (for grader's calibration)\n    21\t\n    22\tFirst, let's summarize the facts that are frequently used in Diophantine equations. We will omit the proofs of well-known facts. The following lemma characterizes Pythagorean triples and is used repeatedly.\n    23\t\n    24\t <Lemma 1> If $a, b, c$ are positive integers with $ab \\neq 0$ satisfying $a^{2}+b^{2}=c^{2}$, then there exist positive integers $m \\neq n$ and $k$ such that\n    25\t\n    26\t \\[\n    27\t (a, b, c)=k\\left(2 m n, m^{2}-n^{2}, m^{2}+n^{2}\\right) \\text { or } k\\left(m^{2}-n^{2}, 2 m n, m^{2}+n^{2}\\right).\n    28\t \\]\n    29\t\n    30\t In particular, if $a, b$ are relatively prime, then $k=1$ and $m$ and $n$ have different parity, and one of $a, b$ is odd and the other is even. The odd one is expressed as $m^{2}-n^{2}$ and the even one is expressed as $2mn$. Also, in this case, $c$ is always odd.\n    31\t <Proof of Lemma 1> Omitted. \\qed\n    32\t <Lemma 2> If $p$ is a prime number or 1, and positive integers $x$ and relatively prime positive integers $a, b$ satisfy\n    33\t\n    34\t \\[\n    35\t p a b=x^{2},\n    36\t \\]\n    37\t\n    38\t then there exist positive integers $u, v$ such that $(a, b, x)=\\left(p u^{2}, v^{2}, p u v\\right)$ or $\\left(u^{2}, p v^{2}, p u v\\right)$.\n    39\t <Proof of Lemma 2> Omitted. \\qed\n    40\t\n    41\t <Lemma 3> If $a^{2}+b^{2}$ is a multiple of 4 for integers $a, b$, then both $a$ and $b$ are even.\n    42\t\n    43\t <Proof of Lemma 3> $a$ and $b$ must have the same parity, but the square of an odd number has a remainder of 1 when divided by 4, so if $a, b$ have the same parity as odd numbers, then $a^{2}+b^{2}$ has a remainder of 2 when divided by 4, so it cannot be a multiple of 4. \\qed\n    44\t\n    45\t Now, let's proceed with the proof of the main problem using these facts.\n    46\t <Step 1> There are no positive integers $x, y, z$ satisfying $x^{4}+y^{4}=z^{2}$. Therefore, $n$ is odd.\n    47\t\n    48\t <Step 1.1> Using proof by contradiction, assume that there exists a positive integer solution $x, y, z$. Among these solutions, choose the solution $(x, y, z)$ with the smallest $z$. If there is a prime number $q$ that divides two of $x, y, z$, then it must also divide the remaining number, and in this case, $\\left(\\frac{x}{q}, \\frac{y}{q}, \\frac{z}{q^{2}}\\right)$ becomes a solution, contradicting the minimality of $z$. Therefore, this solution is pairwise relatively prime.\n    49\t\n    50\t <Step 1.2> Since $\\left(x^{2}, y^{2}, z\\right)$ is a Pythagorean triple, by <Lemma 1>, there exist relatively prime positive integers $u, v$ with different parity such that\n    51\t\n    52\t\n    53\t \\begin{equation*}\n    54\t x^{2}=u^{2}-v^{2}, y^{2}=2 u v, z=u^{2}+v^{2} \\tag{1.1}\n    55\t \\end{equation*}\n    56\t\n    57\t\n    58\t We can assume that $u > v$ without loss of generality. Since $y^{2}=2 u v$ and $u, v$ are relatively prime, by <Lemma 2>, there exist positive integers $s, t$ such that $u=2 s^{2}, v=t^{2}$ or $u=s^{2}, v=2 t^{2}$.\n    59\t\n    60\t (Case 1) $u=2 s^{2}, v=t^{2}$\n    61\t\n    62\t $x^{2}+t^{4}=4 s^{4}$. By <Lemma 3>, both $x$ and $t$ must be even, but from $x^{2}=u^{2}-v^{2}$, $x$ is odd, which is a contradiction.\n    63\t\n    64\t (Case 2) $u=s^{2}, v=2 t^{2}$\n    65\t\n    66\t $x^{2}+4 t^{4}=s^{4}$ and since $t|v, v| y^{2}$,\n    67\t\n    68\t \\[\n    69\t \\operatorname{gcd}(x, t)|\\operatorname{gcd}(x, v)| \\operatorname{gcd}\\left(x, y^{2}\\right)=1.\n    70\t \\]\n    71\t\n    72\t Therefore, $x$ and $t^{2}$ are also relatively prime, and by <Lemma 1>, there exist relatively prime positive integers $q, r$ with different parity (since $x$ is odd by (1.1)) such that\n    73\t\n    74\t \\[\n    75\t x=q^{2}-r^{2}, 2 t^{2}=2 q r, s^{2}=q^{2}+r^{2}.\n    76\t \\]\n    77\t\n    78\t Since $t^{2}=q r$, by <Lemma 2>, there exist positive integers $x', y'$ such that $q=x^{\\prime 2}, r=y^{\\prime 2}$, and thus by the above equation, $x^{\\prime 4}+y^{\\prime 4}=s^{2}$. Since\n    79\t\n    80\t \\[\n    81\t s=\\sqrt{u} \\leq u<u^{2}+v^{2}=z,\n    82\t \\]\n    83\t\n    84\t this contradicts the minimality of $(x, y, z)$. Therefore, the proof is complete.\n    85\t\n    86\t <Step 2> If $p^{3}=a^{4}+b^{4}$, then there exist positive integers $x, y, z$ such that $x^{2}+3 y^{4}=z^{4}$.\n    87\t\n    88\t <Step 2.1> If $p \\mid a$, then clearly $p \\mid b$ must also hold, and vice versa. Therefore, $a, b$ are either both multiples of $p$ or not. However, if the former is the case, the right-hand side becomes a multiple of $p^{4}$, which is a contradiction, so $p$ is relatively prime to both $a$ and $b$.\n    89\t\n    90\t <Step 2.2> Therefore, $p$ is a divisor of the sum of squares, and since $(p, a)=(p, b)=1$, $p$ is a prime number of the form $4 k+1$, so it can be expressed in the form $p=c^{2}+d^{2}$. By Jacobi's theorem, there are only two ways (considering order) to express $p^{3}$ as the sum of perfect squares:\n    91\t\n    92\t \\[\n    93\t (p c)^{2}+(p d)^{2} \\text { and } \\left(c\\left(c^{2}-3 d^{2}\\right)\\right)^{2}+\\left(d\\left(3 c^{2}-d^{2}\\right)\\right)^{2}.\n    94\t \\]\n    95\t\n    96\t By (1), the first case cannot be expressed as $a^{4}+b^{4}$, so\n    97\t\n    98\t \\[\n    99\t a^{2}=\\left|c\\left(c^{2}-3 d^{2}\\right)\\right|, \\quad b^{2}=\\left|d\\left(3 c^{2}-d^{2}\\right)\\right|\n   100\t \\]\n   101\t\n   102\t (or vice versa, but we can ignore this case due to symmetry) must hold. Letting $C=|c|, D=|d|$, we have\n   103\t\n   104\t\n   105\t \\begin{equation*}\n   106\t a^{2} b^{2}=C D \\times\\left|C^{2}-3 D^{2}\\right| \\times\\left|3 C^{2}-D^{2}\\right| \\tag{1.2}\n   107\t \\end{equation*}\n   108\t\n   109\t\n   110\t and $\\operatorname{gcd}(C, D)=1$. Now, we divide into cases depending on whether $C D$ is a multiple of 3.\n   111\t\n   112\t (Case 1) If $3 \\mid C D$\n   113\t\n   114\t Without loss of generality, assume $3 \\mid C$. Then, from\n   115\t\n   116\t \\[\n   117\t \\left(\\frac{a b}{3}\\right)^{2}=\\frac{C}{3} \\cdot D \\cdot\\left|3\\left(\\frac{C}{3}\\right)^{2}-D^{2}\\right| \\cdot\\left|3 C^{2}-D^{2}\\right|,\n   118\t \\]\n   119\t\n   120\t since the four terms are pairwise relatively prime, there exist positive integers $e, f, g, h$ such that\n   121\t\n   122\t \\[\n   123\t C=3 e^{2}, \\quad D=f^{2},\\left|3 e^{4}-f^{4}\\right|=g^{2},\\left|27 e^{4}-f^{4}\\right|=h^{2}.\n   124\t \\]\n   125\t\n   126\t Then, by $\\bmod 3$, we have $g^{2}+3 e^{4}=f^{4}$.\n   127\t\n   128\t (Case 2) If $3 \\nmid CD$\n   129\t\n   130\t From (1.2), since the four terms are pairwise relatively prime, there exist positive integers $e, f, g, h$ such that\n   131\t\n   132\t \\[\n   133\t C=e^{2}, \\quad D=f^{2},\\left|e^{4}-3 f^{4}\\right|=g^{2},\\left|3 e^{4}-f^{4}\\right|=h^{2}.\n   134\t \\]\n   135\t\n   136\t Similarly, by $\\bmod 3$, we have $e^{4}-3 f^{4}=g^{2}$.\n   137\t\n   138\t Therefore, in both cases, there exists a positive integer solution to $x^{2}+3 y^{4}=z^{4}$.\n   139\t\n   140\t <Step 3> There are no positive integers $x, y, z$ satisfying $x^{2}+3 y^{4}=z^{4}$.\n   141\t\n   142\t Using proof by contradiction, assume that there exists a positive integer solution $x, y, z$. Among these solutions, choose the solution $(x, y, z)$ with the smallest $z$. If there is a prime number $q$ that divides two of $x, y, z$, then it must also divide the remaining number, and in this case, $\\left(\\frac{x}{q^{2}}, \\frac{y}{q}, \\frac{z}{q}\\right)$ becomes a solution, contradicting the minimality of $z$. Therefore, this solution is pairwise relatively prime.\n   143\t\n   144\t Now, rewriting the equation as\n   145\t\n   146\t\n   147\t \\begin{equation*}\n   148\t 3 y^{4}=\\left(z^{2}-x\\right)\\left(z^{2}+x\\right) \\tag{1.3}\n   149\t \\end{equation*}\n   150\t\n   151\t\n   152\t we can divide into two cases since $\\operatorname{gcd}\\left(z^{2}-x, z^{2}+x\\right)=1$ or 2.\n   153\t\n   154\t (Case 1) If $x$ and $z$ have the same parity\n   155\t\n   156\t By <Lemma 2>, there exist relatively prime positive integers $u, v$ such that\n   157\t\n   158\t \\[\n   159\t z^{2}-x=2 u^{2}, z^{2}+x=6 v^{2}, y^{2}=2 u v \\quad \\text { or } \\quad z^{2}+x=2 u^{2}, z^{2}-x=6 v^{2}, y^{2}=2 u v.\n   160\t \\]\n   161\t\n   162\t In both cases, we have\n   163\t\n   164\t\n   165\t \\begin{equation*}\n   166\t z^{2}=u^{2}+3 v^{2}, y^{2}=2 u v. \\tag{1.4}\n   167\t \\end{equation*}\n   168\t\n   169\t\n   170\t Since $y$ is even, one of $u, v$ must be even.\n   171\t\n   172\t <Step 3.1> If $u$ is even, then $v$ is odd, and then $z^{2}=u^{2}+3 v^{2} \\equiv 3(\\bmod 4)$, which is a contradiction.\n   173\t\n   174\t <Step 3.2> If $v$ is even, then by <Lemma 2>, there exist positive integers $s, t$ such that\n   175\t\n   176\t \\[\n   177\t u=s^{2}, v=2 t^{2},\n   178\t \\]\n   179\t\n   180\t and substituting this back into (1.4) gives $s^{4}+12 t^{4}=z^{2}$. Here, a perfect power is a number that can be expressed in the form $a^{b}$, where $a$ is a positive integer and $b$ is an integer greater than or equal to 2. Prove that there are infinitely many $n$ such that $A_{n}$ divides $n+2024$.\n    13\t\n    14\t## Reference Solution (for grader's calibration)\n    15\t\n    16\tLet's look at the following lemma using the intermediate value property.\n    17\t\n    18\t <Lemma 1> If a sequence of non-negative integers $\\left(x_{n}\\right)_{n=1}^{\\infty}$ satisfies $x_{n+1}-x_{n} \\in\\{0,1\\}$ and for any $M>0$, there exists a positive integer $n$ such that $\\frac{n}{x_{n}}>M$, then there are infinitely many $n$ such that $\\frac{n}{x_{n}}$ is a positive integer.\n    19\t\n    20\t <Proof of Lemma 1> First, let $k_{0}$ be the smallest $k$ such that $x_{k} \\geq 1$. Now, we will prove that for any integer $m \\geq k_{0}$, there exists an $n$ such that $\\frac{n}{x_{n}}=m$.\n    21\t\n    22\t (1) There exists a positive integer $n$ such that $\\frac{n}{x_{n}}>m$, and let $n_{0}$ be the smallest such $n$. Since $\\frac{k_{0}}{x_{k_{0}}} \\leq k_{0}$, we have $n_{0} \\geq k_{0}+1$.\n    23\t\n    24\t (2) By the minimality of $n_{0}$ and $n_{0} \\geq k_{0}+1$, we have $\\frac{n_{0}-1}{x_{n_{0}-1}} \\leq m$. If $\\frac{n_{0}-1}{x_{n_{0}-1}}<m$, then\n    25\t\n    26\t \\[\n    27\t n_{0}<m x_{n_{0}-1}+1 \\quad \\Rightarrow \\quad n_{0} \\leq m x_{n_{0}-1}.\n    28\t \\]\n    29\t\n    30\t However, from $\\frac{n_{0}}{x_{n_{0}}}>m$, we have $m x_{n_{0}}<n_{0}$, so combining the two results gives\n    31\t\n    32\t \\[\n    33\t m x_{n_{0}-1} \\geq n_{0}>m x_{n_{0}},\n    34\t \\]\n    35\t\n    36\t which leads to $x_{n_{0}-1}>x_{n_{0}}$. This contradicts the condition of the problem that $x_{n+1}-x_{n} \\in\\{0,1\\}$. Therefore, we must have $\\frac{n_{0}-1}{x_{n_{0}-1}}=m$, and the proof is complete.\n    37\t\n    38\t Therefore, for any integer $m \\geq k_{0}$, there exists an $n$ such that $\\frac{n}{x_{n}}=m$, and it is obvious that these values of $n$ are different for different $m$, so the proof is complete. \\qed\n    39\t\n    40\t The following lemma is a Bernoulli-type inequality.\n    41\t\n    42\t <Lemma 2> For any integer $k \\geq 2$, we have $2^{k} \\geq \\frac{k^{2}}{2}$.\n    43\t\n    44\t <Proof of Lemma 2> Since $k \\geq 2$, by the binomial theorem, we have\n    45\t\n    46\t \\[\n    47\t 2^{k} \\geq 1+k+\\binom{k}{2}=1+\\frac{k}{2}+\\frac{k^{2}}{2}>\\frac{k^{2}-k}{2},\n    48\t \\]\n    49\t\n    50\t so the proof is complete. $\\qed$\n    51\t\n    52\t Now, let's prove the main problem.\n    53\t\n    54\t <Step 1> Finding an upper bound for $A_{n}$\n    55\t\n    56\t <Step 1.1> For a positive integer $a$ and $k \\geq 2$, let a number of the form $a^{k}$ be called a $k$-th power. Combining these results gives\n    85\t\n    86\t \\[\n    87\t \\frac{2^{2 k}}{x_{2^{2 k}}} \\geq \\frac{2^{2 k}}{A_{2^{2 k}}} \\geq \\frac{k}{4},\n    88\t \\]\n    89\t\n    90\t so for any $M>0$, there exists an $n$ such that $\\frac{n}{x_{n}}>M$.\n    91\t\n    92\t <Step 2.3> By (1), (2), and <Lemma 1> above, there are infinitely many $n$ such that $x_{n} \\mid n$. Therefore, there are also infinitely many $n$ such that\n    93\t\n    94\t \\[\n    95\t \\frac{n+2024}{A_{n}}=\\frac{n+2024}{x_{n+2024}}\n    96\t \\]\n    97\t\n    98\t is a positive integer.\n    99\t\n   100\t## Grading Guidelines\n   101\t\n   102\t(Partial)\n   103\t 1. Observed that $A_n$ is a slowly increasing sequence, and from there guessed that the sequence satisfied some property such as Lemma 1 of the given solution.\n   104\t(Almost)\n   105\t 1. Applied intermediate value property to get useful information, but couldn't complete the proof.\n   106\t\n   107\t## Candidate Response (to be graded)\n   108\t\n   109\tLet $A_n$ be the number of perfect powers less than or equal to $n$. A perfect power is an integer of the form $a^b$ where $a\\ge 1$ and $b\\ge 2$. We want to prove that there are infinitely many positive integers $n$ such that $A_n$ divides $n+2024$.\n   110\t\n   111\tLet $S$ be the set of perfect powers.
